[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through the given list of numbers.
2. For each number, iterate through the list again and calculate the absolute difference between the current number and every other number.
3. If the absolute difference is less than the given threshold, return True.
4. If no such pair of numbers is found, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def has_close_elements(numbers: List[float], threshold: float) -> bool:
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        for j in range(i+1, len(numbers)):
            if abs(numbers[i] - numbers[j]) < threshold:
                return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to extract separate groups of balanced parentheses from the input string.
2. We can achieve this by iterating over the characters in the string and keeping track of the opening and closing parentheses.
3. Whenever we encounter an opening parenthesis '(', we start a new group and capture characters until we encounter a closing parenthesis ')'.
4. We can ignore any spaces in the input string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
from typing import List

def separate_paren_groups(paren_string: str) -> List[str]:
    result = []
    group = ""
    for char in paren_string:
        if char == '(':
            group = char
        elif char == ')':
            group += char
            result.append(group)
        elif char != ' ':  # Ignore spaces
            group += char
    return result
```

```
from typing import List

def separate_paren_groups(paren_string: str) -> List[str]:
    result = []
    group = ""
    open_count = 0
    for char in paren_string:
        if char == '(':
            open_count += 1
            if open_count == 1:  # Start of a new group
                group = char
        elif char == ')':
            open_count -= 1
            group += char
            if open_count == 0:  # End of the current group
                result.append(group)
        elif char != ' ':  # Ignore spaces
            group += char
    return result
```

from typing import List

def below_zero(operations: List[int]) -> bool:
    balance = 0
    for operation in operations:
        balance += operation
        if balance < 0:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To calculate the Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) for a given list of numbers, we first need to calculate the mean of the numbers.
2. Then, for each number in the list, we calculate the absolute difference between the number and the mean.
3. We take the average of all these absolute differences to get the MAD.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def mean_absolute_deviation(numbers: List[float]) -> float:
    # Calculate the mean of the numbers
    mean = sum(numbers) / len(numbers)
    
    # Calculate the absolute differences and store them in a list
    abs_diff = [abs(num - mean) for num in numbers]
    
    # Calculate the average of the absolute differences
    mad = sum(abs_diff) / len(abs_diff)
    
    return mad
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `intersperse` that takes two arguments - a list of integers `numbers` and an integer `delimeter`.
2. Initialize an empty list to store the result.
3. Iterate through the input list `numbers` and for each element:
    - Append the element to the result list.
    - If the current element is not the last element in the list, append the `delimeter` to the result list.
4. Return the result list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def intersperse(numbers: List[int], delimeter: int) -> List[int]:
    result = []
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        result.append(numbers[i])
        if i != len(numbers) - 1:
            result.append(delimeter)
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to parse the input string which contains multiple groups of nested parentheses separated by spaces.
2. We will iterate through each group and calculate the maximum level of nesting of parentheses in that group.
3. To calculate the maximum level of nesting, we can keep track of the current nesting level while iterating through the characters of the group.
4. We can use a stack to keep track of the nesting level. Whenever we encounter an opening parenthesis '(', we increment the nesting level by 1, and whenever we encounter a closing parenthesis ')', we decrement the nesting level by 1.
5. The maximum value of the nesting level during the iteration will give us the deepest level of nesting of parentheses in that group.
6. We will repeat this process for each group in the input string and store the deepest level of nesting for each group in a list.
7. Finally, we will return the list containing the deepest levels of nesting for each group.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def parse_nested_parens(paren_string: str) -> List[int]:
    def max_nesting_level(group):
        max_level = 0
        current_level = 0
        for char in group:
            if char == '(':
                current_level += 1
                max_level = max(max_level, current_level)
            elif char == ')':
                current_level -= 1
        return max_level

    groups = paren_string.split()
    result = [max_nesting_level(group) for group in groups]
    return result
```

            filtered_strings.append(string)
    return filtered_strings

from typing import List, Tuple

def sum_product(numbers: List[int]) -> Tuple[int, int]:
    sum_result = 0
    product_result = 1
    
    for num in numbers:
        sum_result += num
        product_result *= num
    
    return sum_result, product_result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the rolling maximum elements.
2. Iterate through the input list of numbers.
3. At each iteration, find the maximum element from the current element and all previous elements.
4. Append this maximum element to the rolling maximum list.
5. Return the rolling maximum list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rolling_max(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    rolling_max_list = []
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        rolling_max_list.append(max(numbers[:i+1]))
    return rolling_max_list
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the shortest palindrome that begins with the supplied string, we need to follow the algorithm provided in the function description.
2. Find the longest postfix of the supplied string that is a palindrome.
3. Append to the end of the string the reverse of the string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string.
    Algorithm idea is simple:
    - Find the longest postfix of supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string reverse of a string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.
    """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i-1][::-1]
```

def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string.
    Algorithm idea is simple:
    - Find the longest postfix of supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string reverse of a string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.
    """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    if is_palindrome(string):
        return string + string[:-1][::-1]

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i-1][::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string.
    Algorithm idea is simple:
    - Check if the whole string is a palindrome, return string concatenated with reverse of all characters except the last one.
    - Find the longest postfix of supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string reverse of a string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.
    """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    if is_palindrome(string):
        return string + string[:-1][::-1]

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i-1][::-1]

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string.
    Algorithm idea is simple:
    - Check if the whole string is a palindrome, return string concatenated with reverse of all characters except the last one.
    - Find the longest postfix of supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string reverse of a string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.
    """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    if is_palindrome(string):
        return string + string[:-1][::-1]

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i][::-1]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given two binary strings `a` and `b`.
2. We need to perform XOR operation on these two strings.
3. XOR operation compares corresponding bits of two operands and returns 1 if they are different, and 0 if they are the same.
4. We will iterate through both strings and perform XOR operation on each pair of corresponding bits.
5. Finally, we will return the result as a binary string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def string_xor(a: str, b: str) -> str:
    result = ''
    for char_a, char_b in zip(a, b):
        if char_a != char_b:
            result += '1'
        else:
            result += '0'
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input list is empty. If it is empty, return None.
2. Iterate over the list of strings and keep track of the longest string seen so far.
3. If we encounter a string that is longer than the current longest string, update the longest string.
4. If we encounter a string of the same length as the current longest string, keep the current longest string (as we need to return the first one in case of a tie).
5. After iterating through all strings, return the longest string found.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List, Optional

def longest(strings: List[str]) -> Optional[str]:
    if not strings:
        return None
    
    longest_string = strings[0]
    
    for string in strings:
        if len(string) > len(longest_string):
            longest_string = string
            
    return longest_string
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the greatest common divisor (GCD) of two integers `a` and `b`.
2. We can use the Euclidean algorithm to find the GCD of two numbers, which states that the GCD of two numbers `a` and `b` is the same as the GCD of `b` and `a % b`.
3. We will keep applying this algorithm recursively until `b` becomes 0, at which point `a` will be the GCD.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def greatest_common_divisor(a: int, b: int) -> int:
    while b != 0:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a
```

    }

    return result

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find how many times a given substring can be found in the original string, considering overlapping cases.
2. We can achieve this by iterating over the original string with a sliding window approach.
3. At each step, we check if the substring matches the current window and increment a counter if it matches.
4. Return the final counter as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def how_many_times(string: str, substring: str) -> int:
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(string) - len(substring) + 1):
        if string[i:i + len(substring)] == substring:
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map each word numeral to its integer equivalent.
2. Split the input `numbers` string by spaces to get a list of word numerals.
3. Convert each word numeral to its integer equivalent using the dictionary from step 1.
4. Sort the list of integers in ascending order.
5. Map the sorted integers back to their word numeral representations.
6. Join the sorted word numerals with spaces to form the final sorted string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def sort_numbers(numbers: str) -> str:
    # Dictionary to map word numerals to integers
    numeral_mapping = {
        'zero': 0,
        'one': 1,
        'two': 2,
        'three': 3,
        'four': 4,
        'five': 5,
        'six': 6,
        'seven': 7,
        'eight': 8,
        'nine': 9
    }
    
    # Split the input string by spaces to get a list of word numerals
    numerals = numbers.split()
    
    # Convert word numerals to integers and sort
    sorted_numerals = sorted([numeral_mapping[numeral] for numeral in numerals])
    
    # Map sorted integers back to word numerals
    sorted_words = [key for value, key in sorted([(numeral_mapping[numeral], numeral) for numeral in numerals])]
    
    # Join sorted word numerals with spaces
    return ' '.join(sorted_words)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the two closest elements in the list, we need to iterate over the list and keep track of the minimum difference between any two numbers.
2. Initialize variables to store the closest pair of numbers and the minimum difference.
3. Iterate over the list and compare the difference between each pair of numbers. Update the closest pair and minimum difference if a smaller difference is found.
4. Return the closest pair in ascending order.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List, Tuple

def find_closest_elements(numbers: List[float]) -> Tuple[float, float]:
    min_diff = float('inf')
    closest_pair = None
    
    for i in range(len(numbers)-1):
        for j in range(i+1, len(numbers)):
            diff = abs(numbers[i] - numbers[j])
            if diff < min_diff:
                min_diff = diff
                closest_pair = (min(numbers[i], numbers[j]), max(numbers[i], numbers[j]))
    
    return closest_pair
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Find the minimum and maximum values from the list of numbers.
2. Calculate the range of values by subtracting the minimum from the maximum.
3. For each number in the list, calculate the rescaled value using the formula: `(number - minimum) / range`.
4. Append the rescaled values to a new list.
5. Return the new list containing rescaled values.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rescale_to_unit(numbers: List[float]) -> List[float]:
    minimum_val = min(numbers)
    maximum_val = max(numbers)
    range_val = maximum_val - minimum_val
    rescaled_numbers = [(num - minimum_val) / range_val for num in numbers]
    return rescaled_numbers
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the prime factors.
2. Start dividing the input number `n` by 2 until it is divisible by 2. Add 2 to the list of prime factors each time it is divided by 2.
3. After dividing by 2, start dividing by 3 until it is divisible by 3. Add 3 to the list of prime factors each time it is divided by 3.
4. Continue this process for increasing odd integers until `n` becomes 1.
5. Return the list of prime factors.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def factorize(n: int) -> List[int]:
    factors = []
    divisor = 2
    while n > 1:
        if n % divisor == 0:
            factors.append(divisor)
            n //= divisor
        else:
            divisor += 1
    return factors
```

from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    unique_elements = []
    for num in numbers:

    return unique_elements

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The error message indicates that the test case `remove_duplicates([1, 2, 3, 2, 4, 3, 5]) == [1, 4, 5]` failed.
2. The current implementation only removes duplicates, but it does not preserve the order of elements as required by the function description.
3. To fix the issue, we need to modify the implementation to maintain the order of elements while removing duplicates.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    unique_elements = []
    seen = set()
    
    for num in numbers:
        if num not in seen:
            unique_elements.append(num)
            seen.add(num)
    
    return unique_elements
```

### Code
```
from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """ From a list of integers, remove all elements that occur more than once.
    Keep order of elements left the same as in the input.
    >>> remove_duplicates([1, 2, 3, 2, 4])
    [1, 3, 4]
    """
    unique_elements = []
    seen = set()
    
    for num in numbers:
        if num not in seen:
            seen.add(num)
            unique_elements.append(num)
    
    return unique_elements
```

1. Iterate through each character in the given string.
2. Check if the character is lowercase or uppercase.
3. If it is lowercase, convert it to uppercase and vice versa.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def flip_case(string: str) -> str:
    flipped_string = ""
    for char in string:
        if char.islower():
            flipped_string += char.upper()
        elif char.isupper():
            flipped_string += char.lower()
        else:
            flipped_string += char
    return flipped_string
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input number `n` is less than 2. If it is, return False because prime numbers start from 2.
2. Loop from 2 to the square root of `n` (inclusive). Check if `n` is divisible by any number in this range.
3. If `n` is divisible by any number in the range, return False because it is not a prime number.
4. If the loop completes without finding any divisors, return True because `n` is a prime number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
import math

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            return False
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The `find_zero` function aims to find a zero point of a polynomial function defined by the coefficients provided in the input list `xs`.
2. The function should only work with lists having an even number of coefficients, and the largest non-zero coefficient should be guaranteed to have a solution.
3. To find the zero point of a polynomial, we need to solve for x such that the polynomial function evaluates to 0.
4. We can use numerical methods like the Newton-Raphson method to find the zero point of the function.
5. We can initialize an initial guess for the zero point and iteratively refine it until we find a point where the polynomial function evaluates to a value close to 0.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def poly(xs: list, x: float):
    """
    Evaluates polynomial with coefficients xs at point x.
    return xs[0] + xs[1] * x + xs[1] * x^2 + .... xs[n] * x^n
    """
    return sum([coeff * math.pow(x, i) for i, coeff in enumerate(xs)])

def find_zero(xs: list):
    if len(xs) % 2 != 0:
        raise ValueError("Number of coefficients must be even")
    
    if max(xs) == 0:
        raise ValueError("Largest non-zero coefficient must be guaranteed")
    
    # Initial guess for zero point
    guess = 0.0
    
    # Newton-Raphson iteration to find zero point
    while True:
        derivative = poly([i * xs[i] for i in range(1, len(xs))], guess)
        next_guess = guess - poly(xs, guess) / derivative
        
        if abs(next_guess - guess) < 0.0001:
            return next_guess
        
        guess = next_guess
```

### Code
```python
def sort_third(l: list):
    indices_divisible_by_3 = [i for i in range(len(l)) if i % 3 == 0]
    sorted_values = [l[i] for i in indices_divisible_by_3]
    sorted_values.sort()
    
    output = [sorted_values.pop(0) if i % 3 == 0 else l[i] for i in range(len(l))]
    return output
```

    return count

3. If the index is even, extract the elements at the even indices of the input list `l`, sort them, and append the sorted values to the result list.

### Requirements

### Code
```python
def sort_even(l: list):
    result = []
    for i in range(len(l)):
        if i % 2 == 0:
            result.extend(sorted([l[j] for j in range(len(l)) if j % 2 == 0]))
        else:
            result.append(l[i])
    return result
```

def sort_even(l: list):
    result = l.copy()  # Create a copy of the original list to avoid modifying the input list
    for i in range(0, len(l), 2):  # Iterate over even indices
        result[i] = sorted(l[i:i+2])  # Sort the elements at even indices
    return [elem for sublist in result for elem in (sublist if isinstance(sublist, list) else [sublist])]  # Flatten the list

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse the encoding process performed by the `encode_cyclic` function.
2. In the `encode_cyclic` function, the input string is split into groups of three characters and each group is cyclically shifted by one position to the left.
3. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse this cyclic left shift operation.
4. We can achieve this by cyclically shifting each group of three characters in the opposite direction (one position to the right) to obtain the original characters.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    returns encoded string by cycling groups of three characters.
    """
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # cycle elements in each group. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[1:] + group[0]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)


def decode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    takes as input string encoded with encode_cyclic function. Returns decoded string.
    """
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # reverse cycle elements in each group. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[-1] + group[:-1]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to generate Fibonacci numbers until we find the n-th number that is both a Fibonacci number and a prime number.
2. To generate Fibonacci numbers, we can start with the first two numbers 0 and 1, and then keep adding the previous two numbers to generate the next number.
3. We need a function to check if a number is prime or not. A number is prime if it is greater than 1 and has no divisors other than 1 and itself.
4. We will iterate through Fibonacci numbers, check if each number is prime, and keep track of how many prime Fibonacci numbers we have found.
5. Once we find the n-th prime Fibonacci number, we return that number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num <= 1:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_fib(n: int):
    count = 0
    a, b = 0, 1
    while count < n:
        c = a + b
        if is_prime(c):
            count += 1
        a, b = b, c
    return c
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find three distinct elements in the input list that sum to zero.
2. To solve this problem, we will use a nested loop to iterate through all possible triplets of elements from the list.
3. For each triplet, we will check if the sum of the three elements is equal to zero.
4. If we find any such triplet, we will return True. If no such triplet is found, we will return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def triples_sum_to_zero(l: list):
    for i in range(len(l)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(l)):
            for k in range(j + 1, len(l)):
                if l[i] + l[j] + l[k] == 0:
                    return True
    return False
```

def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right;  simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left.   The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other.  All cars move in the same speed.  Two cars are said to collide
    when a car that's moving left to right hits a car that's moving right to left.
    However, the cars are infinitely sturdy and strong; as a result, they continue moving
    in their trajectory as if they did not collide.

    This function outputs the number of such collisions.
    """
    return n // 2  # Each pair of cars from opposite directions will collide, hence divide by 2

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to iterate through the list of integers.

3. To do this efficiently, we can use a set to keep track of the elements seen so far.
4. If the negative of the current element is present in the set, then we have found two distinct elements that sum to zero.
5. Return True if such a pair is found, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def pairs_sum_to_zero(l):
    seen = set()
    for num in l:
        if -num in seen:
            return True
        seen.add(num)
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `change_base` that takes two parameters `x` and `base`.
2. Convert the integer input number `x` to the desired base using the given `base`.
3. Return the string representation of the converted number.
4. Make sure the base is less than 10 as per the function description.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def change_base(x: int, base: int):
    if not isinstance(x, int) or not isinstance(base, int) or base < 2 or base > 9:
        return "Invalid input. Base must be an integer between 2 and 9."
    
    if x == 0:
        return '0'
    
    result = ''
    while x > 0:
        remainder = x % base
        result = str(remainder) + result
        x = x // base
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to create a function to efficiently compute the n-th element of the Fib4 number sequence.
2. We cannot use recursion, so we need to use an iterative approach.
3. We will initialize a list to store the first four elements of the sequence.
4. Then we will iterate from 4 to n and calculate each subsequent element using the formula `fib4(n) = fib4(n-1) + fib4(n-2) + fib4(n-3) + fib4(n-4)`.
5. Finally, we return the n-th element of the Fib4 number sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fib4(n: int):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    if n == 2:
        return 2
    if n == 3:
        return 0
    
    fib_nums = [0, 0, 2, 0]
    
    for i in range(4, n+1):
        fib_nums[i % 4] = fib_nums[0] + fib_nums[1] + fib_nums[2] + fib_nums[3]
    
    return fib_nums[n % 4]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Sort the input list `l` in ascending order.
2. Check if the length of the list is odd or even.
3. If the length is odd, return the middle element.
4. If the length is even, return the average of the two middle elements.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def median(l: list):
    l_sorted = sorted(l)
    n = len(l_sorted)
    if n % 2 == 1:
        return l_sorted[n // 2]
    else:
        return (l_sorted[n // 2 - 1] + l_sorted[n // 2]) / 2
```

### Code
```
def encode_shift(s: str):
    """
    returns encoded string by shifting every character by 5 in the alphabet.
    """
    return "".join([chr(((ord(ch) + 5 - ord("a")) % 26) + ord("a")) for ch in s])

def decode_shift(s: str):
    """
    takes as input string encoded with encode_shift function. Returns decoded string.
    """
    return "".join([chr(((ord(ch) - 5 - ord("a")) % 26) + ord("a")) for ch in s])
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to calculate the n-th Fibonacci number based on the input value of n.
2. Fibonacci sequence starts with 0, 1 and each subsequent number is the sum of the two preceding ones.
3. We can use a recursive approach or an iterative approach to calculate the n-th Fibonacci number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def fib(n: int):
    if n <= 0:
        return "Invalid input. Please provide a positive integer greater than 0."
    elif n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 1
    else:
        a, b = 0, 1
        for _ in range(2, n):
            a, b = b, a + b
        return b
```

def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    open_count = 0
    close_count = 0
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == '<':
            open_count += 1
        elif bracket == '>':
            close_count += 1
    
    return open_count == close_count

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The current implementation counts the number of opening and closing brackets separately.
2. It then checks if the count of opening brackets is equal to the count of closing brackets to determine if the bracketing is correct.
3. The issue arises when there is an unexpected closing bracket before an opening bracket.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == '<':
            stack.append(bracket)
        elif bracket == '>':
            if not stack:
                return False
            stack.pop()
    
    return len(stack) == 0
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if the elements in the list are monotonically increasing or decreasing.
2. To do this, we can iterate through the list and compare each element with the next one.
3. If all elements are either in non-decreasing order or non-increasing order, the list is considered monotonic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def monotonic(l: list):
    increasing = all(l[i] <= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    decreasing = all(l[i] >= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    
    return increasing or decreasing
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the common elements between two lists.
2. First, we will convert the lists into sets to get unique elements from each list.
3. Then, we will find the intersection of the two sets to get the common elements.
4. Finally, we will return the sorted common elements as a list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def common(l1: list, l2: list):
    set1 = set(l1)
    set2 = set(l2)
    
    common_elements = list(set1.intersection(set2))
    common_elements.sort()
    
    return common_elements
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through numbers starting from 2 (smallest prime number) up to the square root of n.
2. For each number, check if it is a factor of n.
3. If a number is a factor of n, check if it is a prime number.
4. Keep track of the largest prime factor found.
5. Return the largest prime factor.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            if is_prime(i):
                largest_prime = i
    return largest_prime

# Test cases
print(largest_prime_factor(13195))  # Output: 29
print(largest_prime_factor(2048))   # Output: 2
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The error message indicates that for the input `15`, the function is returning `5` instead of the correct largest prime factor.

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    i = 2
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i:
            i += 1
        else:
            n //= i
            largest_prime = i
    if n > largest_prime:
        largest_prime = n
    return largest_prime

# Test cases
print(largest_prime_factor(13195))  # Output: 29
print(largest_prime_factor(2048))   # Output: 2
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if every opening bracket has a corresponding closing bracket in the given string of brackets.
2. We can achieve this by iterating through each character in the string.
3. We will use a stack to keep track of opening brackets.
4. If we encounter an opening bracket, we push it onto the stack.
5. If we encounter a closing bracket, we check if the stack is empty (meaning no opening bracket for the closing bracket) or the top of the stack is not an opening bracket. In this case, we return False.
6. If all brackets are correctly paired, the stack should be empty at the end.
7. Return True if the stack is empty, else return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == '(':
            stack.append(bracket)
        elif bracket == ')':
            if not stack or stack[-1] != '(':
                return False
            stack.pop()
    
    return len(stack) == 0
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The derivative of a polynomial term ax^n is n * ax^(n-1).
2. Iterate through the list of coefficients, skipping the first element (constant term) as it does not affect the derivative.
3. For each coefficient at index i, multiply it by i to get the derivative coefficient.
4. Append the derivative coefficient to a new list.
5. Return the new list as the derivative of the polynomial.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def derivative(xs: list):
    derivative_coeffs = []
    for i in range(1, len(xs)):
        derivative_coeffs.append(xs[i] * i)
    return derivative_coeffs
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input `n` is equal to 0, 1, or 2. If so, return the corresponding values (0, 0, 1) as per the definition of the FibFib sequence.
2. If `n` is greater than 2, use a loop to calculate the FibFib sequence up to the `n-th` element using the formula `fibfib(n) = fibfib(n-1) + fibfib(n-2) + fibfib(n-3)`.

### Code
```python
def fibfib(n: int):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    elif n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 1
    else:
        fibfib_seq = [0, 0, 1]
        for i in range(3, n+1):
            fib_n = fibfib_seq[i-1] + fibfib_seq[i-2] + fibfib_seq[i-3]
            fibfib_seq.append(fib_n)
        return fibfib_seq[n]
```

### Code
```
def vowels_count(s):
    vowels = ['a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u']
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i].lower() in vowels:
            count += 1
        elif i == len(s) - 1 and s[i].lower() == 'y':
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the integer x to a string to easily manipulate the digits.
2. Calculate the number of digits in x.
3. If shift is greater than the number of digits, reverse the string and return the result.
4. Otherwise, perform a circular shift by shifting the digits to the right by shift.
5. Return the result as a string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def circular_shift(x, shift):
    x_str = str(x)
    num_digits = len(x_str)
    
    if shift > num_digits:
        return x_str[::-1]
    
    shift %= num_digits
    shifted_str = x_str[-shift:] + x_str[:-shift]
    
    return shifted_str
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given a string as input and we need to find the sum of the ASCII codes of the uppercase characters in the input string.
2. Iterate over each character in the input string.
3. Check if the character is an uppercase character using `isupper()` method.
4. If it is an uppercase character, find its ASCII code using `ord()` function and add it to the sum.
5. Finally, return the sum of ASCII codes of all uppercase characters in the string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def digitSum(s):
    ascii_sum = 0
    for char in s:
        if char.isupper():
            ascii_sum += ord(char)
    return ascii_sum
```

### Code
```python
def fruit_distribution(s, n):
    # Split the string to extract the number of apples and oranges
    fruits = s.split()
    apples = int(fruits[0])
    oranges = int(fruits[3])
    
    # Calculate the total number of mango fruits
    mango = n - apples - oranges
    
    return mango
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize variables to keep track of the smallest even value and its index.
2. Iterate through the array and check if the current element is even and smaller than the current smallest even value.
3. If the condition is met, update the smallest even value and its index.
4. If there are multiple nodes with the same smallest even value, choose the one with the smallest index.
5. Return the smallest even value and its index in a list format.
6. If there are no even values or the array is empty, return an empty list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def pluck(arr):
    smallest_even = float('inf')
    smallest_even_index = -1
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        if num % 2 == 0 and num < smallest_even:
            smallest_even = num
            smallest_even_index = i
    
    if smallest_even_index == -1:
        return []
    
    return [smallest_even, smallest_even_index]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a dictionary to store the frequency of each integer in the input list.
2. Iterate through the input list and count the frequency of each integer by updating the dictionary.
3. Iterate over the dictionary items and find the greatest integer that satisfies the condition of having a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself.
4. If such an integer is found, return it. If no such integer is found, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def search(lst):
    frequency_map = {}
    
    # Count the frequency of each integer in the input list
    for num in lst:
        if num in frequency_map:
            frequency_map[num] += 1
        else:
            frequency_map[num] = 1
    
    # Find the greatest integer that satisfies the condition
    greatest_integer = -1
    for num, freq in frequency_map.items():
        if num > greatest_integer and freq >= num:
            greatest_integer = num
    
    return greatest_integer
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. First, we need to sort the given list of integers in ascending order.
2. Then, we can create a new list where we iterate through the sorted list and alternatively append the minimum and maximum values from the sorted list to the new list.
3. Finally, return the new list as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def strange_sort_list(lst):
    if not lst:
        return []
    
    sorted_lst = sorted(lst)
    result = []
    
    min_idx, max_idx = 0, len(sorted_lst) - 1
    
    while min_idx <= max_idx:
        result.append(sorted_lst[min_idx])
        if min_idx != max_idx:
            result.append(sorted_lst[max_idx])
        min_idx += 1
        max_idx -= 1
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given three sides can form a valid triangle by verifying whether the sum of any two sides is greater than the third side.
2. If the sides form a valid triangle, calculate the area using Heron's formula.
3. Heron's formula to calculate the area of a triangle given its three sides: 
   - Let `a`, `b`, and `c` be the three sides of the triangle.
   - Calculate the semi-perimeter, `s`, using the formula: `s = (a + b + c) / 2`
   - Calculate the area using the formula: `Area = √(s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c))`.
4. Round the calculated area to 2 decimal points.
5. If the sides do not form a valid triangle, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def triangle_area(a, b, c):
    # Check if the three sides form a valid triangle
    if a + b > c and a + c > b and b + c > a:
        # Calculate the semi-perimeter
        s = (a + b + c) / 2
        # Calculate the area using Heron's formula
        area = math.sqrt(s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c))
        # Round the area to 2 decimal points
        return round(area, 2)
    else:
        return -1
```

3. Check if the sum of elements in the list `q` is less than or equal to the maximum possible weight `w`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def will_it_fly(q, w):
    if q == q[::-1]:  # Check if the list is palindromic
        if sum(q) <= w:  # Check if sum of elements is less than or equal to w
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the minimum number of elements that need to be changed to make the array palindromic, we can follow these steps:
2. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of changes needed.
3. Loop through the array from the start and end simultaneously until they meet in the middle.
4. For each pair of elements at the corresponding positions, if they are not equal, increment `count` by 1.
5. The final `count` will be the minimum number of changes needed to make the array palindromic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def smallest_change(arr):
    count = 0
    n = len(arr)
    for i in range(n // 2):
        if arr[i] != arr[n - 1 - i]:
            count += 1
    return count
```

3. If the total number of characters in the second list is less than the total number of characters in the first list, return the second list.
4. If the total number of characters in both lists are equal, return the first list.
5. To calculate the total number of characters in a list of strings, we need to iterate over each string in the list and sum up the lengths of all the strings.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def total_match(lst1, lst2):
    total_chars_lst1 = sum(len(word) for word in lst1)
    total_chars_lst2 = sum(len(word) for word in lst2)
    
    if total_chars_lst1 < total_chars_lst2:
        return lst1
    elif total_chars_lst2 < total_chars_lst1:
        return lst2
    else:
        return lst1
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if the given number is a product of exactly 3 prime numbers.
2. We can find all prime numbers less than 100, and then check all possible combinations of 3 prime numbers to see if their product is equal to the given number.
3. To find all prime numbers less than 100, we can use the Sieve of Eratosthenes algorithm.
4. Once we have a list of prime numbers, we can generate all possible combinations of 3 primes and check if their product is equal to the given number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def generate_primes():
    primes = []
    for num in range(2, 100):
        if is_prime(num):
            primes.append(num)
    return primes

def is_multiply_prime(a):
    primes = generate_primes()
    for i in range(len(primes)):
        for j in range(i+1, len(primes)):
            for k in range(j+1, len(primes)):
                if primes[i] * primes[j] * primes[k] == a:
                    return True
    return False
```

    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False

from itertools import combinations

def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def generate_primes():
    primes = []
    for num in range(2, 100):
        if is_prime(num):
            primes.append(num)
    return primes

def is_multiply_prime(a):
    primes = generate_primes()
    prime_combinations = combinations(primes, 3)
    for prime_set in prime_combinations:
        if prime_set[0] * prime_set[1] * prime_set[2] == a:
            if len(set(prime_set)) == 3:  # Ensure distinct prime numbers
                return True
    return False

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To determine if a number `x` is a simple power of another number `n`, we need to check if `n` raised to an integer power results in `x`.
2. We can iterate through integer powers starting from 1 until `x` and check if `n**i == x` for any `i`.
3. If at any point `n**i == x`, we return True. If we finish iterating and do not find such an `i`, we return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_simple_power(x, n):
    if x == 1:
        return n == 1
    for i in range(1, x):
        if n**i == x:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To determine if an integer is a cube of some integer number, we need to find the cube root of the given integer.
2. We can use the cube root function to check if the cube root of the given integer is an integer.
3. If the cube root is an integer, then the given integer is a cube of some integer number and we can return True, else return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def iscube(a):
    if a < 0:
        a = -a
    cube_root = round(a ** (1/3))
    return cube_root ** 3 == a
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function `is_prime` to check if a number is prime by iterating from 2 to the square root of the number and checking if the number is divisible by any number in that range.
2. Define a function `hex_key` that takes a hexadecimal number as input.
3. Create a variable `prime_count` to keep track of the count of prime hexadecimal digits.
4. Iterate through each character in the input hexadecimal number.
5. For each character:
    - Convert it to a decimal integer.
    - Check if the decimal integer is a prime number.
    - If it is a prime number and it is one of the specified digits (2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13), increment the `prime_count`.
6. Return the `prime_count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def hex_key(num):
    prime_digits = set([2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13])
    prime_count = 0
    for char in num:
        decimal_value = int(char, 16)
        if is_prime(decimal_value) and decimal_value in prime_digits:
            prime_count += 1
    return prime_count
```

3. We need to remove the '0b' prefix and add the 'db' characters at the beginning and end of the binary string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def decimal_to_binary(decimal):
    binary = bin(decimal)[2:]  # Convert to binary and remove '0b' prefix
    return 'db' + binary + 'db'  # Add 'db' characters at the beginning and end

1. Check if the length of the string is less than 3, return `False` as per the condition.
2. Iterate through the string with a step size of 3 to access every 3 consecutive letters.
3. Check if all 3 consecutive letters are distinct using a set to store unique characters. If any character is repeated, return `False`.
4. If the loop completes without returning `False`, return `True`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_happy(s):
    if len(s) < 3:
        return False
    
    for i in range(0, len(s)-2, 3):
        if len(set(s[i:i+3])) != 3:
            return False
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The initial implementation of the `is_happy` function checks if the length of the string is less than 3 and returns `False`.
2. It then iterates through the string in steps of 3 to access every 3 consecutive letters.
3. For each set of 3 consecutive letters, it checks if they are distinct by converting them into a set and comparing the length. If any character is repeated, it returns `False`.
4. If the loop completes without finding any repeated characters in the 3 consecutive letters, it returns `True`.

The error occurred because the implementation only checks for distinct characters in sets of 3 consecutive letters. It doesn't handle cases where the first and last characters of the string may be the same but not consecutive.

To fix this issue, we need to adjust the logic to consider the first and last characters separately if the string length is greater than or equal to 3.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def is_happy(s):
    if len(s) < 3:
        return False
    
    if len(s) == 3:
        return len(set(s)) == 3
    
    for i in range(0, len(s)-2, 3):
        if len(set(s[i:i+3])) != 3:
            return False

    if len(s) % 3 == 1:
        return len(set(s[:2])) == 2 and len(set(s[-2:])) == 2
    elif len(s) % 3 == 2:
        return len(set(s[-3:])) == 3

    return True
```

def numerical_letter_grade(grades):
    letter_grades = []
    for gpa in grades:
        if gpa == 4.0:
            letter_grades.append('A+')
        elif gpa > 3.7:
            letter_grades.append('A')
        elif gpa > 3.3:
            letter_grades.append('A-')
        elif gpa > 3.0:
            letter_grades.append('B+')
        elif gpa > 2.7:
            letter_grades.append('B')
        elif gpa > 2.3:
            letter_grades.append('B-')
        elif gpa > 2.0:
            letter_grades.append('C+')
        elif gpa > 1.7:
            letter_grades.append('C')
        elif gpa > 1.3:
            letter_grades.append('C-')
        elif gpa > 1.0:
            letter_grades.append('D+')
        elif gpa > 0.7:
            letter_grades.append('D')
        elif gpa > 0.0:
            letter_grades.append('D-')
        else:
            letter_grades.append('E')
    return letter_grades
```

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_length(string):
    length = len(string)
    return is_prime(length)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the count of n-digit positive integers that start or end with 1.
2. Iterate through all n-digit positive integers.
3. For each integer, check if it starts with 1 or ends with 1.
4. If it starts or ends with 1, increment the `count`.
5. Return the final `count` as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def starts_one_ends(n):
    count = 0
    for i in range(10**(n-1), 10**n):
        if str(i)[0] == '1' or str(i)[-1] == '1':
            count += 1
    return count
```

def add(lst):
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, len(lst), 2):
        if lst[i] % 2 == 0:
            total += lst[i]
    return total

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string into words based on space.
2. For each word, sort the characters in ascending order based on ASCII value.
3. Join the sorted words back together with space to form the final ordered string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def anti_shuffle(s):
    words = s.split()
    ordered_words = []
    for word in words:
        ordered_chars = ''.join(sorted(word))
        ordered_words.append(ordered_chars)
    return ' '.join(ordered_words)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through each row in the 2-dimensional list.
2. For each row, check if the integer `x` is present in that row.
3. If `x` is present in the row, store the coordinates (row index, column index of `x`) in a list of tuples.
4. Sort the list of tuples by rows in ascending order.
5. Sort the coordinates of each row by columns in descending order.
6. Return the sorted list of tuples.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_row(lst, x):
    coordinates = []
    
    for i, row in enumerate(lst):
        for j, val in enumerate(row):
            if val == x:
                coordinates.append((i, j))
    
    coordinates.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], -x[1]))
    
    return coordinates
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the length of the given array is 0, if so return an empty array.
2. Calculate the sum of the first and last index values of the array.
3. If the sum is odd, return a sorted copy of the array in ascending order.
4. If the sum is even, return a sorted copy of the array in descending order.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(array):
    if len(array) == 0:
        return []
    
    first_last_sum = array[0] + array[-1]
    if first_last_sum % 2 == 1:
        return sorted(array)
    else:
        return sorted(array, reverse=True)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map the original alphabet to the rotated alphabet where each letter is shifted down by two multiplied by two places.
2. Iterate through each character in the input string and check if it is an alphabet character.
3. If the character is an alphabet character, find its corresponding rotated character from the dictionary and append it to the encrypted string.
4. If the character is not an alphabet character, simply append it as it is to the encrypted string.
5. Return the encrypted string as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encrypt(s):
    alphabet = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    rotated_alphabet = alphabet[4:] + alphabet[:4]  # Rotate alphabet down by 2 multiplied by 2 places
    
    alphabet_dict = dict(zip(alphabet, rotated_alphabet))
    
    encrypted_string = ''
    for char in s:
        if char.isalpha():
            encrypted_string += alphabet_dict[char]
        else:
            encrypted_string += char
    
    return encrypted_string
```

def next_smallest(lst):
    unique_sorted_lst = sorted(set(lst))  # Remove duplicates and sort the list
    if len(unique_sorted_lst) < 2:
        return None
    return unique_sorted_lst[1]

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def any_int(x, y, z):
    if isinstance(x, int) and isinstance(y, int) and isinstance(z, int):
        if x == y + z or y == x + z or z == x + y:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map vowels to the letter that appears 2 places ahead of them in the English alphabet.
2. Iterate through each character in the message.
3. If the character is a letter, swap its case.
4. If the character is a vowel, replace it with the corresponding value from the dictionary.
5. Return the modified message.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encode(message):
    vowels = {'a': 'c', 'e': 'g', 'i': 'k', 'o': 'q', 'u': 'w', 'A': 'C', 'E': 'G', 'I': 'K', 'O': 'Q', 'U': 'W'}
    encoded_message = ''
    for char in message:
        if char.isalpha():
            if char.lower() in vowels:
                encoded_message += vowels[char.lower()]
            else:
                encoded_message += char.swapcase()
        else:
            encoded_message += char
    return encoded_message
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a helper function `is_prime(num)` to check if a number is prime or not.
2. Iterate through the given list of integers.
3. For each integer, check if it is prime using the `is_prime` helper function.
4. If the integer is prime, find the sum of its digits and keep track of the largest prime value found so far.
5. Finally, return the sum of digits of the largest prime value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(n):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(n))

    largest_prime = None
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            if largest_prime is None or num > largest_prime:
                largest_prime = num

    return digit_sum(largest_prime) if largest_prime is not None else 0
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given dictionary is empty, if it is then return False.
2. Extract all the keys from the dictionary.
3. Check if all keys are either all lower case strings or all upper case strings.
4. If all keys are in either lower or upper case, return True. Otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def check_dict_case(dict):
    if not dict:
        return False
    
    keys = dict.keys()
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False
```

def check_dict_case(dict):
    if not dict:
        return False
    
    keys = [key for key in dict.keys() if isinstance(key, str)]
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False

1. The error message indicates that the test case `check_dict_case({"p":"pineapple", 5:"banana", "a":"apple"})` failed and should return `False`.
2. The previous implementation did not handle the case where there are duplicate keys in the dictionary. 
3. To fix the issue, we need to update the logic to account for duplicate keys in the dictionary.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def check_dict_case(input_dict):
    if not input_dict:
        return False
    
    keys = [key for key in input_dict.keys() if isinstance(key, str)]
    
    if len(set(keys)) != len(keys):
        return False
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a helper function `is_prime(num)` that takes a number as input and returns `True` if the number is prime, otherwise `False`.
2. Create an empty list `primes` to store prime numbers less than `n`.
3. Iterate from 2 to `n` (exclusive) using a for loop.
4. Check if the current number is prime using the `is_prime` function.
5. If the number is prime, append it to the `primes` list.
6. Return the `primes` list as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def count_up_to(n):
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n):
        if is_prime(i):
            primes.append(i)
    return primes
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Extract the unit digit of both integers by taking the modulus 10 of each number.
2. Multiply the unit digits obtained in step 1.
3. Return the result of the multiplication.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def multiply(a, b):
    unit_digit_a = abs(a) % 10
    unit_digit_b = abs(b) % 10
    return unit_digit_a * unit_digit_b
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the count of uppercase vowels at even indices.
2. Create a list of uppercase vowels ['A', 'E', 'I', 'O', 'U'].
3. Iterate over the characters in the string `s` at even indices (indices 0, 2, 4, etc.).
4. Check if the character is an uppercase vowel and increment the `count` if it is.
5. Return the final value of `count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def count_upper(s):
    count = 0
    vowels = ['A', 'E', 'I', 'O', 'U']
    
    for i in range(0, len(s), 2):
        if s[i] in vowels:
            count += 1
            
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the input `value` which is a string representing a number to a float to handle decimal values.
2. Calculate the floor and ceiling of the float value.
3. Check which integer is closer to the original number.
4. If the number is equidistant from two integers, round it away from zero by checking if the decimal part is greater than or equal to 0.5.
5. Return the closest integer.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def closest_integer(value):
    num = float(value)
    floor_num = int(num)
    ceil_num = int(num) + 1
    if num - floor_num >= 0.5:
        return ceil_num
    elif num - floor_num < 0.5:
        return floor_num
```

### Code
```
def closest_integer(value):
    num = float(value)
    floor_num = int(num)
    ceil_num = int(num) + 1
    decimal_part = num - int(num)
    
    if decimal_part >= 0.5:
        return ceil_num
    elif decimal_part < -0.5:
        return floor_num - 1
    else:
        return floor_num
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the number of stones in each level.
2. Append the initial number of stones (n) to the list.
3. For the remaining levels:
    - If the current number of stones is odd, the next level will have the next odd number of stones.
    - If the current number of stones is even, the next level will have the next even number of stones.
4. Append the calculated number of stones for each level to the list.
5. Repeat step 3 until reaching n levels.
6. Return the list of stones in each level.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def make_a_pile(n):
    stones_per_level = [n]  # Initial level with n stones
    for i in range(n-1):  # Calculate stones for each level
        if stones_per_level[-1] % 2 == 0:  # Check if the current number of stones is even
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 1)  # Next level has next odd number of stones
        else:
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 2)  # Next level has next even number of stones
    return stones_per_level
```

    stones_per_level = [n]  # Initial level with n stones

        if stones_per_level[-1] % 2 == 0:  # Check if the current number of stones is even

def make_a_pile(n):
    stones_per_level = [n]  # Initial level with n stones
    for i in range(n-1):  # Calculate stones for each level
        if stones_per_level[-1] % 2 == 0:  # Check if the current number of stones is even
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 2)  # Next level has next even number of stones
        else:
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 2)  # Next level has next even number of stones for both cases (odd and even)
    return stones_per_level
```

### Code
```
def choose_num(x, y):
    max_even = -1
    for num in range(x, y+1):
        if num % 2 == 0 and num > max_even:
            max_even = num
    return max_even
```

4. Convert the rounded average to binary.
5. Return the binary representation as a string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def rounded_avg(n, m):
    if n > m:
        return -1
    avg = round((n + m) / 2)
    return bin(avg)
```

### Code
```
def unique_digits(x):
    def has_even_digit(num):
        for digit in str(num):
            if int(digit) % 2 == 0:
                return True
        return False
    
    result = [num for num in x if not has_even_digit(num)]
    
    return sorted(result)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Sort the array to have integers between 1 and 9 inclusive.
2. Reverse the sorted array.
3. Replace each digit by its corresponding name from "One", "Two", "Three", "Four", "Five", "Six", "Seven", "Eight", "Nine".
4. If the array is empty, return an empty array.
5. If the array has any strange number, ignore it.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def by_length(arr):
    if not arr:  # Check if the array is empty
        return []
    
    # Filter out integers between 1 and 9 inclusive
    filtered_arr = [x for x in arr if 1 <= x <= 9]
    
    # Sort the filtered array
    sorted_arr = sorted(filtered_arr)
    
    # Reverse the sorted array
    reversed_arr = sorted_arr[::-1]
    
    # Map each digit to its corresponding name
    digits_map = {1: "One", 2: "Two", 3: "Three", 4: "Four", 5: "Five", 6: "Six", 7: "Seven", 8: "Eight", 9: "Nine"}
    result = [digits_map[x] for x in reversed_arr]
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create an empty list to store the output.
2. Iterate over the range from 1 to n (inclusive).
3. For each index i:
   - If i is even, calculate the factorial of i.
   - If i is odd, calculate the sum of numbers from 1 to i.
4. Append the result to the output list.
5. Return the output list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def f(n):
    def factorial(num):
        if num == 0:
            return 1
        else:
            return num * factorial(num - 1)
        
    result = []
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if i % 2 == 0:
            result.append(factorial(i))
        else:
            result.append(sum(range(1, i+1)))
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize two variables, `even_count` and `odd_count`, to keep track of the number of even and odd palindromes respectively.
2. Iterate through numbers from 1 to n (inclusive).
3. For each number, check if it is a palindrome.
4. If it is a palindrome, check if it is even or odd and increment the respective count.
5. Return a tuple containing the counts of even and odd palindromes.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(num):
    return str(num) == str(num)[::-1]

def even_odd_palindrome(n):
    even_count = 0
    odd_count = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if is_palindrome(i):
            if i % 2 == 0:
                even_count += 1
            else:
                odd_count += 1
                
    return (even_count, odd_count)
```

2. Iterate through each number in the input array.

4. If the sum of digits is greater than 0, increment the `count`.
5. Finally, return the `count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def count_nums(arr):
    count = 0
    for num in arr:
        if num < 0:
            num = -num
            digits = [int(d) for d in str(num)]
            if sum(digits) > 0:
                count += 1
        else:
            digits = [int(d) for d in str(num)]
            if sum(digits) > 0:
                count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given array is empty. If it is empty, return True as the array is already sorted.
2. Find the index of the minimum element in the array.
3. Check if the array is already sorted in non-decreasing order. If it is sorted, return True.
4. Determine the number of right shifts required to move the minimum element to the beginning of the array.
5. Check if the array becomes sorted after performing the required number of right shifts. If it does, return True; otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def move_one_ball(arr):
    if not arr:
        return True
    
    min_index = arr.index(min(arr))
    if arr == sorted(arr):
        return True
    
    shifts_required = len(arr) - min_index
    new_arr = arr[-shifts_required:] + arr[:-shifts_required]
    
    return new_arr == sorted(arr)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if it is possible to exchange elements between `lst1` and `lst2` to make all elements in `lst1` even.
2. We can achieve this by simply checking if the sum of odd numbers in `lst1` is less than or equal to the sum of even numbers in `lst2`. If this condition is satisfied, then it is possible to exchange elements to make all elements in `lst1` even.
3. If the condition is met, we return "YES", otherwise we return "NO".

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def exchange(lst1, lst2):
    sum_odd_lst1 = sum(x for x in lst1 if x % 2 != 0)
    sum_even_lst2 = sum(x for x in lst2 if x % 2 == 0)
    
    if sum_odd_lst1 <= sum_even_lst2:
        return "YES"
    else:
        return "NO"
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string by space to get individual letters.
2. Create a dictionary to store the count of each letter.
3. Iterate through the letters and update the count in the dictionary.
4. Find the maximum count value in the dictionary.
5. Create a new dictionary containing only the letters with the maximum count value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
            
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The input string should be split by space to get individual letters.
2. Create a dictionary to store the count of each letter.
3. Iterate through the letters and update the count in the dictionary.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
    
    if not letter_count:  # Check if the dictionary is empty
        return {}
    
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Remove all characters in string `s` that are present in string `c`.
2. Check if the resulting string is a palindrome.
3. Return a tuple containing the result string and True/False for the palindrome check.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def reverse_delete(s, c):
    # Remove characters in s that are present in c
    result = ''.join(char for char in s if char not in c)
    
    # Check if the result is a palindrome
    is_palindrome = result == result[::-1]
    
    return result, is_palindrome
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the minimum sum of any non-empty sub-array within the given array of integers.
2. We can achieve this by iterating through all possible sub-arrays and calculating their sums.
3. Initialize a variable to store the minimum sum and set it to a large value initially.
4. Iterate through all possible sub-arrays using nested loops.
5. For each sub-array, calculate the sum and update the minimum sum if the current sum is smaller.
6. Return the minimum sum found.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def minSubArraySum(nums):
    min_sum = float('inf')
    for i in range(len(nums)):
        current_sum = 0
        for j in range(i, len(nums)):
            current_sum += nums[j]
            min_sum = min(min_sum, current_sum)
    return min_sum
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to iterate over each well in the grid and calculate the amount of water in each well.
2. We will keep extracting water from the well until it becomes empty.
3. We will keep track of the total number of times we need to lower the buckets.
4. Finally, we will return the total count of times the buckets were lowered.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowerings = 0
    
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            while well > 0:
                well -= capacity
                total_lowerings += 1
    
    return total_lowerings
```

def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowerings = 0
    
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            total_lowerings += max(0, well - capacity)
    
    return total_lowerings
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to sort the given array of non-negative integers based on the number of ones in their binary representation in ascending order.
2. If two numbers have the same number of ones in their binary representation, we need to sort them based on their decimal value.
3. We will first define a custom sorting key function that calculates the number of ones in the binary representation of a number and then the number itself.
4. We will use this custom sorting key function with the `sorted` function to sort the array.
5. We will return the sorted array as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(arr):
    def custom_sort_key(num):
        return bin(num).count('1'), num
    return sorted(arr, key=custom_sort_key)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string `s` into individual words.
2. For each word, count the number of consonants in it.
3. If the count of consonants in a word is equal to the input `n`, then add that word to the result list.
4. Return the list of words that contain exactly `n` consonants.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def select_words(s, n):
    def count_consonants(word):
        consonants = set("bcdfghjklmnpqrstvwxyzBCDFGHJKLMNPQRSTVWXYZ")
        return sum(1 for letter in word if letter in consonants)

    words = s.split()
    result = [word for word in words if count_consonants(word) == n]
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given a list of two strings containing only open and close parentheses.
2. We need to check if it is possible to concatenate the two strings in some order to form a good string with balanced parentheses.
3. To achieve this, we can simulate the scenario by tracking the count of open and close parentheses.
4. We can iterate through both strings and increment the count for open parentheses and decrement the count for close parentheses.
5. If at any point the count becomes negative or the final count is not zero, then it is not possible to form a good string.
6. If the count remains non-negative and becomes zero at the end, then it is possible to form a good string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def match_parens(lst):
    open_count = 0
    close_count = 0

    for string in lst:
        for char in string:
            if char == '(':
                open_count += 1
            elif char == ')':
                if open_count > 0:
                    open_count -= 1
                else:
                    return 'No'
    
    return 'Yes' if open_count == 0 else 'No'

# Test cases
print(match_parens(['()(', ')']))  # Output: 'Yes'
print(match_parens([')', ')']))     # Output: 'No'
```

```python
def match_parens(lst):
    open_count = 0

    concat_string = ''.join(lst)  # Concatenate the strings in the list
    
    for char in concat_string:
        if char == '(':
            open_count += 1
        elif char == ')':
            if open_count > 0:
                open_count -= 1
            else:
                return 'No'
    
    return 'Yes' if open_count == 0 else 'No'

### Code
```
def match_parens(lst):
    open_count = 0
    
    for i in range(len(lst)):
        for char in lst[i]:
            if char == '(':
                open_count += 1
            elif char == ')':
                if open_count > 0:
                    open_count -= 1
                else:
                    return 'No'
        
        if i < len(lst) - 1:
            if open_count == 0:
                return 'Yes'
    
    return 'Yes' if open_count == 0 else 'No'
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To solve this problem, we need to find the k maximum elements in the given array `arr`.
2. We can achieve this by sorting the array in descending order and returning the first k elements.
3. If k is 0, we can simply return an empty list.
4. If k is equal to the length of the array, we can return the sorted array as is.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def maximum(arr, k):
    arr.sort(reverse=True)
    return arr[:k]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.
2. Start with the given positive integer `n`.
3. Calculate the Collatz sequence for `n` by following the rules:
    - If the number is odd, multiply it by 3 and add 1.
    - If the number is even, divide it by 2.
    - Repeat these steps until the sequence reaches 1.
4. While calculating the sequence, keep track of the odd numbers encountered.
5. Sort the odd numbers in increasing order.
6. Return the sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    odd_collatz = []
    while n != 1:
        if n % 2 == 0:
            n = n // 2
        else:
            odd_collatz.append(n)
            n = 3 * n + 1
    odd_collatz.append(1)  # Add 1 to the sequence
    return sorted(odd_collatz)
```
